EDITORIAL QUALITY CRITERIA

Vitruvio journal declares its commitment to respect the integrity of published works. For this reason, plagiarism is strictly
prohibited and texts that are identified as either plagiarism or fraudulent content will be removed or unpublished. The
journal will act in this cases as quickly as possible. By accepting the terms and agreements expressed by our journal,
authors must ensure that the text and the materials associated with it are original (or that they have the publishing per-
missions) and that there isn’t infringed copyright. Authors also have to justify that in case of joint authorship, there was
full consensus of all authors involved and that the manuscript has not been previously presented or published in other
media.

Expressed opinions are those of the authors and do not necessarily reflect the editorial opinion. All people listed as au-
thors are responsible for the contents of the investigation by having contributed substantially in their physical execution
and/or intellectually. Authorship is recognized for the contributions made to the field of knowledge, therefore, the author
is responsible for agreeing with the team that took part in the conduct, and for the decision about the order of the authors.

Acknowledgements should be cited, explaining the sources of research funding (if any) and aid, substantial advice or
recommendations made by third parties.

EDITORIAL
Ecological transition and cultural transformation:
from roots to innovation

“Before even thinking about form or function, I try to understand the atmosphere, listen to the place and let myself be guided by
what it suggests”, reveals architect Werner Tscholl in the interview that opens this issue of Vitruvio Journal.

It is the place itself, with its layered history, its materials and its morphology, that suggests the direction of the project.
The willingness to listen to the genius loci represents a profound cultural attitude that recognises existing heritage not as a
constraint, but as an intellectual resource at the heart of an innovative project.

Werner Tscholl, born in Laces in Val Venosta in 1955, has made the principle of “building on the built” the foundation
of his design philosophy, the result of a deep understanding of the territorial limitations imposed by the Alpine context of
South Tyrol in Italy. Architect Tscholl has transformed this limitation into his own vision of the world and of design: not a
renunciation, but an opportunity to reinterpret buildings by placing them in continuity with the present, preserving what
the architect himself defines as “the soul of places”. In his architecture, sustainability does not derive from the application of
standardised technical protocols, but from an overall cultural vision that integrates memory, identity, territorial belonging,
traditional building knowledge and technological innovation.

To overcome the environmental problem, it is necessary to understand and analyse its causes. As G. Bateson pointed
out as early as 1972, “the ecological crisis is a crisis of thought” (Steps to an Ecology of Mind, 1972) and any lasting solution
must necessarily address the cultural roots that have generated the current unsustainable model. D. Meadows identifies a
correlation between “system” and “value”, according to which “systems cannot be understood unless the values that guide
them are understood” (Thinking in Systems, 2008). “The advent of culture has brought with it cultural evolution, a superorganic
mode of evolution superimposed on the organic mode, and which in the last few millennia has become the dominant mode of
human evolution” - Francisco J. Ayala (In the Light of Evolution II: Biodiversity and Extinction, 2008). For this reason, ecological
transition requires a change in cultural paradigms: from domination to care, from extraction to regeneration, from unlimited
growth to sufficiency.

Change requires a re-education of society in terms of the value of resources, translating scientific complexity into forms
of knowledge that are accessible, meaningful and participatory for the community. E. Morin speaks of the need for a “reform
of thought” that links complexity, responsibility and solidarity (La Voie, Pour l’avenir de l’humanité, 2011), central elements
in addressing global environmental challenges. Ecology thus becomes a shared culture, capable of guiding behaviour and
political decisions, the driving force and matrix within which forms of life, value systems and ways of relating to the environ-
ment are configured. According to anthropologist F. Facchini, “(...) The relationship between man and culture is so close that
culture itself represents man’s environment, indeed his ecological niche” (Ominizzazione, cultura, umanizzazione, 2005). The
knowledge of accumulated knowledge is, in fact, the basis for its dissemination, enhancement, adaptation and develop-
ment. Only in this way, can innovation take root in the local context while respecting collective intelligence and the identity
and soul of the place.

The research published in this issue of Vitruvio Journal highlights that ecological transition in architecture cannot be
reduced to the performance of the built environment (however necessary this aspect may be), but occurs when the project
becomes a driver of innovation through a coherent cultural vision: respect for the history and memory of places; the recog-
nition of territorial and environmental limits; the responsible use of local and circular resources; the construction of spaces
that respond to contemporary needs without losing a sense of belonging and identity. The disciplines of design, increasingly
aware of the environmental and socio-cultural complexity of the present, find themselves operating at the intersection of
different temporalities, scales and knowledge. Therefore, architecture becomes a lens through which to read the relation-
ship between society and ecosystems, between material heritage and future prospects, between handed-down knowledge
and new analytical and design tools. This means affirming that the knowledge built up over time by a local community has

2 V

<!-- p. 9 -->

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

value, that the construction practices developed in vernacular architecture respond to a logic of optimisation with respect
to specific environmental, climatic and material conditions, and that this accumulated knowledge represents a heritage of
collective intelligence from which to draw consciously.

Memory is a part of culture that dynamically connects different times: it bears witness to the past, supports the present
and guides the future, allowing identity to manifest itself, be transmitted and evolve. The research by Fernández Tapia et al.
documents in 3D the air-raid shelters of Alcalá de Henares and Sigüenza, underground spaces that housed collective fear
during the bombings of the Spanish Civil War, transforming these environments into vectors of contemporary understanding,
into spaces where present and future generations can read, study and reinterpret that historical experience. Time is also the
fundamental dimension of meanwhile urbanism (De León Rodríguez & Temes-Cordovez): suspended spaces such as vacant
lots, interstitial areas, territories awaiting future development. Temporariness, from a transitory condition, becomes a design
resource. Temporary urbanism practices show how community activation, flexible land use and the creation of social and
environmental micro-ecologies are effective strategies for regenerating areas that have remained on the margins of formal
urban processes, strengthening the identity of the place, civic participation and collective awareness of landscape values.

In Ergün’s research, the reinterpretation of a traditional element of the Arab world (mashrabiya) as a contemporary climate device shows how innovation can arise from tradition when it is reinterpreted with advanced analytical tools. The
article quantifies the effectiveness of mashrabiya in reducing cooling loads, confirming that the heritage of vernacular climate strategies represents a largely unexplored design archive. The present allows us to reinterpret the past through new
perspectives with the aim of obtaining detailed information that will advance our knowledge. In the research by AlHarbat et
al., text-to-image AI platforms are used to reinterpret the Ottoman style in religious architecture. The quality of the images
produced by six platforms is evaluated using morphological and semantic criteria, showing how the definition of the prompt
radically influences the architectural result. This opens a debate on the responsibility and awareness that such tools impose.

Starting from the intangible culture of vernacular construction techniques, it is possible to investigate possibilities for
innovation that respond to today’s increasingly complex needs, creating a cultural line of continuity that enhances the local
area. Research on bousillage (Minguito García) shows how an ancient construction technique, based on wooden structures
and plant fillings, can find new forms of relevance in the ecological transition. The three variants analysed, with Spanish
moss, golden broom and Posidonia oceanica, demonstrate the ability of natural materials to offer thermo-hygrometric and
environmental performance suited to contemporary needs and, at the same time, to respond to the demand for sustainability in construction. The conscious and systematic enhancement of local territorial resources produces a dual synergistic
effect of great importance for the ecological transition: the development of circular economies anchored to the territory and,
simultaneously, an increase in widespread ecological awareness in the community. These two effects are inseparable, as
represent two complementary aspects of the same process of cultural and material transformation. When a local community
consciously understands that the resources used in its construction or production chains come from the territory it inhabits
and that the sustainable management of these resources determines the environmental quality of the place itself, a form
of radical and concrete ecological awareness emerges. In the IMIP project (Gilabert-Sanz & Oliver-Villanueva), prefabricated
modular panel systems were tested, made through the strategic use of local territorial resources (wood, cork), structuring a
transition methodology based on the principles of “Transition Design”.

Intervention on existing built heritage is now an imperative necessity and a valuable opportunity. The crucial challenge
lies in carrying out interventions on buildings while maintaining a balance between the need for speed and scalability in the
implementation of solutions, the achievement of adequate performance standards and respect for the architectural quality,
historical identity and cultural values inherent in built heritage. The research by Iborra Lucas et al. develops an integrated
methodology that combines structural analysis and energy simulation to assess the technical feasibility of industrialised
façade systems in obsolete buildings. Their approach, which is compatible with the circular economy and life cycle analysis,
extends the useful life of the building stock and reduces its overall environmental impact. Onecha & Suárez propose an innovative methodology for calculating the embodied carbon footprint of historic buildings, analysing three Catalan modernist
houses built between 1900 and 1917. Their contribution challenges the widespread perception that historic buildings are
inherently unsustainable, demonstrating that their embodied carbon footprint is comparable (or lower) to that of contemporary buildings.

In this context, architect Werner Tscholl’s invitation becomes a necessity: “I believe that the main strategy is to build less,
but with more culture: to prioritise the quality and meaning of the project over quantity”.
Stefania De Gregorio

V 3

<!-- p. 11 -->

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Werner Tscholl. Photo credits: Manuel Ferrigato.

Werner Tscholl stands out as a key figure in contemporary Alpine architecture. His practice is characterized by a coherent
integration of territorial analysis, structural control, and a critical engagement with the existing built environment. His
production, primarily concentrated in Alto Adige–Südtirol (Italy), is based on a methodological approach grounded in the
reading of context from a cultural, morphological, and structural point of view. This approach is evident in his projects,
where pre-existing elements and new additions are always clearly distinguished, and construction solutions are thoughtfully adapted to the specific topography and climate conditions.

In his projects — from infrastructure and productive buildings, to interventions on historic complexes — Tscholl employs
spatial and material systems that preserve the legibility of stratification, while delivering functional efficiency, durability,
and a strong integration with the landscape. This attitude underpins a design approach focused on typological coherence
and on a notion of sustainability understood as a balanced and responsible relationship between architecture, traditions,
and contemporary use.

The following interview examines the theoretical and practical foundations of his work, beginning with the formative
context of Val Venosta, which constitutes the geographical and cultural background from which his design methodology
has evolved.

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025) Werner Tscholl
International Journal of Architecture Interview
Technology and Sustainability

Luis Palmero, Graziella Bernardo

INTERVIEW WITH WERNER TSCHOLL

Valencia, 21/11/2025

LP/GB: You were born and raised in Val Venosta, an Alpine
landscape with a very strong local identity… What traces
has that territory left in the way you look at architecture
and space?

Werner Tscholl: Certainly the Romanesque landscape,
which is still very present today in the upper part of Val
Venosta. It is an essential, sober landscape, in which
buildings, materials, and forms arise from a direct rela-
tionship with the territory and with available resources.
This has probably refined in me a sensitivity toward a
measured architecture, rooted in context and capable of
dialoguing with the environment without imposing itself
upon it.

Another important trace derives from the fact that, during
my youth, the valley was still quite poor. This condition
implied a careful and responsible use of means, a certain
frugality, and a culture of “making do with little”, which
was also reflected in the way buildings were constructed
and spaces inhabited. I believe this experience contrib-
uted to shaping my view of architecture, orienting it
toward simple but meaningful solutions, in which every
choice carries weight and necessity.

After this initial approach to architecture, your education
took you to Florence, one of the cradles of art and spatial
thinking in Europe.

LP/GB: How did your vocation for architecture emerge,
and how did your period of study in Florence — surrounded
by art, history, and the Renaissance — influence your
formation?

WT: As strange as it may seem, my vocation for archi-
tecture arose almost suddenly. Until then, I had never
shown any particular talent for drawing, and after school
my dream was to become a film director. However,
I missed enrollment at the University of Munich and
would have had to wait a year before starting that path. Figure 1 | Monte Maria-Marienberg abbey entrance. Photo credits: Renè Riller.
During that waiting period, the idea of studying archi-
tecture arrived like a true lightning bolt.

The choice of Florence was equally spontaneous,
linked to the memory of a school experience in San architecture, and more because of the extraordinary
Gimignano that had deeply impressed me. My years freedom of thought that the 1970s offered us students.
of study in Florence played a fundamental role in my Despite the chaos and strikes, professors and assis-
formation, perhaps less because of being immersed tants were always available for those who, like me, truly
daily in a city so rich in art, history, and Renaissance wanted to learn.

intuitively, but once identified they are pursued coher-

LP/GB: Your interventions on historic heritage — from
defensive structures to rural settlements — show a balance
between respect and transformation.

What criteria guide your choices when working on a
building or a stratified context? When intervening in places
marked by important memories, where is the threshold
beyond which transformation risks becoming loss?

WT: When intervening in a historic building or a stratified
context, the fundamental principle is to position oneself
as a temporary user rather than an owner. Before building
something new, one must assess whether an equiva-
lent or even superior architecture can emerge from the
existing fabric, enhancing the history already embedded
in the heritage. The threshold beyond which an interven-
tion risks becoming harmful is reached when the new
erases or trivializes the memory of the place.

Our approach is clear: the old remains old, the new
becomes new and must speak the language of our
time. Transforming means making stratifications legible,
strengthening their meaning, and adding a contemporary
layer without denying what already exists. Different mate-
rials are also used to distinguish the new from the old, so
that each intervention is clearly recognizable and datable,
allowing future generations to understand it and, if neces-
sary, modify it without losing history. Respect for heritage
is not immobility, but awareness: knowing when to inter-
vene decisively and when instead to step back, inserting
works that are authentically of our time.

LP/GB: Your architecture is characterized by a skilled and
innovative use of materials such as steel, concrete, and
wood, often in dialogue with local building traditions.

How do you approach the choice of materials in relation
to durability and environmental impact?

WT: The choice of materials is an integral part of the
project’s original idea and not a subsequent decision.
Each material is closely linked to the form and “Gestalt”
of the building, contributing to defining its dialogue with
the context, both visually and culturally. Durability and
sustainability are not afterthoughts, but intrinsic qualities
of the choice itself: a material coherent with place, climate,
and building tradition naturally tends to be longer-lasting
and more responsible.

WT: I believe the main strategy is to build less, but with
more culture: prioritizing quality and meaning over
quantity. In Alpine areas, it is often more effective to
create light, targeted infrastructure, such as cable cars,
rather than new roads that profoundly alter the land-
scape. Building in the mountains is always a delicate
challenge: architecture must show great empathy in
recognizing the soul of a place without compromising
it. The most sustainable approach is to build as little as
possible; when intervention is necessary, the building
must enter into dialogue with the landscape and
become a form of enhancement.

It is essential to establish clear limits on where and
when to build, respecting the natural characteristics
and environmental sensitivities of the territory. Moving
away from an idea of luxury as an end in itself, toward
a concept of essential experience — where enjoyment
of the landscape, the mountains, and nature prevails
— allows for the promotion of sustainable tourism that
enriches the place without compromising its ecolog-
ical balance.

From a technical standpoint, building in the mountains
has become easier than in the past. Where once work
was done mainly by hand, with all the slowness and
modesty this entailed, today machinery is used, often
with questionable results. For this reason, every inter-
vention must combine technical innovation with deep
respect for context, in order to be sustainable, harmo-
nious, and authentically rooted in the territory.

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Air raid shelters as tangible cultural heritage:
analysis and strategies for their study and 3D
documentation

Enrique Fernández Tapia  , Diego Martín de Torres  , Juan Luis Bermúdez-González  *,
Jorge Luis López-Viejo

1Department of Architecture, Universidad de Alcalá (Spain)
\*Email:   luis.bermudez@uah.es

Abstract: Public air raid shelters, which emerged as a response to massive bombardment of the civilian
population, were put to the test for the first time in history during the Spanish Civil War. This sample of
defensive architecture represents a unique type of tangible cultural heritage with a high associated
significance. Taking as a case study two significant shelters in the central peninsular area, this article analyses
their historical evolution, their architectural characteristics and, especially, the possibilities offered by their
documentation using 3D technologies. It evaluates the physical, technical and contextual particularities that
condition their documentation, conservation and enhancement. The uniqueness of its architecture and the
underground context, presents many challenges when it comes to studying and documenting it. However,
the new three-dimensional documentation technologies have reached a sufficient degree of maturity to allow
their study in a generalised manner and the obtaining of precise and expressive documentation, which not
only facilitates their architectural study, but also their dissemination and valorisation, as spaces charged with
historical memory.

Keywords: Military heritage; war architecture; air raid shelters; 3D documentation; Alcalá de Henares;
Sigüenza.

Received: 23/07/2025; Accepted: 18/11/2025; Available online: 11/12/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.
Cite as: Fernández Tapia, E., Martín de Torres, D., Bermúdez-González, J.L., López-Viejo, J.L. (2025). ‘Air raid shelters as tangible cultural heritage: analysis
and strategies for their study and 3D documentation’. VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24369.
https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24369

Aspects to consider Comments
\1. Depth and type of access Access often requires climbing or speleology gear.
\2. State of preservation Usable inside, though partly ruined or filled with rubble.
\3. Safety conditions Possible toxic gases, risk of collapse, falling debris, underground shafts.
\4. Existing lighting Portable lighting needed for navigation and photography
\5. Presence of water Consider waterproof gear and dry spots to place equipment.
\6. Surfaces Smooth, uniform surfaces hinder photos and scanning.
\7. Layout of galleries Complex layouts require multiple scan positions.
\8. Width of galleries Sets minimum photo/scan distance (>60 cm).
\9. Gallery height Low gallery height increases working time inside
\10. Existence of shafts Documenting vertical shafts requires tripods or lifting systems.
\11. Time availability Limited time restricts planning and technology use.
\12. Singular elements Some elements need to be documented in a special way.

The results obtained for the cloud set are displayed in
Figure 8, with none of the individual links demonstrating
an overlap of less than 35% or a link strength of less than
60%. Once the clouds were calculated and processed to
provide a single data set, the models were subjected to a
process of refinement, whereby superfluous points, such
as those pertaining to human presence or water reflec-
tions, in addition to noise, were eliminated. The Leica
Cyclone Register 360 software was utilised to establish a
connection between the clouds and the cleaning process.

During this phase, the point cloud is also linked to GPS
control points on the ground for georeferencing. The
process entailed the identification of points on the outer
cloud situated beyond the confines of the gallery for
which GPS coordinates had been obtained. The resultant
Figure 4 | Air raid shelter located under Alameda Park in Sigüenza georeferenced point cloud exhibited an absolute posi-
(Guadalajara). (Source: by the authors). tioning error of less than seven centimetres in the XYZ

\6. Discussion

These structures, built in a context of war, not only repre-
sent the material response to a threat, but also bear Figure 12 | Current state of one of the entrances to the shelter at the
witness to human experiences, in many cases extreme Barberán y Collar Airfield. (Source: authors, 2025).

that are still alive. This is why it poses important chal-
lenges for study, understanding and valorisation.

The underground nature of these shelters presents unique
challenges for their study and conservation. The difficulty
of access and the abandoned conditions in which they
are often found complicate, in turn, any work inside.

The working methodology and choice of equipment,
lighting, control techniques, post-processing and 3D
cloud visualisation applied in this research are based on
previous experience in documenting qanats — an ancient
Arab technique for collecting and transporting water using
wells and underground galleries. At the same time, the
distinctive characteristics of underground shelters have
helped improve existing 3D documentation processes.

There is a very close interconnection between air-raid
shelters and this collective memory, as these infrastruc-
tures are a particularly expressive material witness of
these moments in history, both in terms of the war context
in general and the experiences related to each shelter in
particular.

To ensure that air-raid shelters are recognised and
valued as cultural heritage, it is essential to implement
active management strategies. This includes research,
documentation, conservation and dissemination of
these spaces, as well as educating society about their
historical significance. The valorisation of these shel-
ters can not only contribute to the preservation of
historical memory but can also foster cultural tourism
and heritage and tourism education (González et al.,
2022).

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Between waiting and project: a comparative
assessment of eight meanwhile urbanism

Ruth De León Rodríguez  *1, Rafael Temes-Cordovez  2

1PhD candidate in Architecture, Building, Heritage and City, Universitat Politècnica de València.
2Department of Urbanism, Universitat Politècnica de València.
\*Email:   rutdeleo@doctor.upv.es

Abstract: This article defines temporary urbanism as a set of practices that activate vacant land by testing
programs, rules, and alliances before their consolidation. It distinguishes between two main interpretations:
the first, “meanwhile urbanism,” seeks to provide a solution during the transitional period in an interrupted
urban process by assigning temporary uses and activities until the planned future development takes place.
The second approach views time as a process of adaptation and demonstration to reach a more robust and
community-driven consensus.
Focusing on “meanwhile urbanism,” the study analyses eight European case studies and identifies four
main typologies: 1. Community-led participatory uses on small plots; 2. Interventions creating temporary
recreational spaces with environmental and educational value; 3. Projects using modular infrastructures to
address equipment or service deficits;4. Large-scale developments incorporating temporary uses to maintain
social engagement during long-term implementation.
The results confirm that community participation, flexible design, and local appropriation are critical to
strengthening social fabric and place identity. These interventions, varying greatly in duration, demonstrate
that interim uses can adapt without obstructing final projects.
The article concludes that temporary urbanism should be regarded as a dynamic complement to formal urban
planning and could be very useful in the Valencian Community where extensive undeveloped lands exist.

Keywords: temporary urbanism; meanwhile urbanism; vacant land reuse; interim planning; tactical urbanism;
community participation.

Received: 07/10/2025; Accepted: 28/11/2025; Available online: 22/12/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: De León Rodríguez, R., & Temes-Cordovez, R. (2025). ‘Between waiting and project: a comparative assessment of eight meanwhile urbanism’.
VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24784. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24784

contrast, Stalled Spaces, Caserres, and Pla BUITS arise
from citizen initiatives or public-private partnerships
focused on the community. For instance, the Caserres
running track emerged through a bottom-up process
with no significant public funding, whereas Fredericia
C was city-led with philanthropic support. Both
approaches aim to activate underused spaces, but
their structures differ: citizen-driven projects prioritize
self-management and shared responsibility (minimal
funding, neighbourhood-led administration), while
institutional models tend to have greater strategic
vision and resources (e.g., Stuttgart allocated €5
million to marketing in Aspern).

• Duration of interventions: There is considerable
temporal variability. Initiatives such as King’s Cross
(2015-2016) and Caserres (active for less than one year
in 2021) are extremely short-lived, whereas programs
like Stalled Spaces and Aspern Seestadt have extended
for over a decade. Plug N’ Play (2008-2015) lasted
around seven years before dismantling, showing that
interim uses can adapt without obstructing long-term
plans. On the other hand, some “interim” initiatives
have exceeded expectations in duration: Tempelhof
remains open as a park since 2010, and Pla BUITS
continues to manage sites years after its inception
(albeit with reduced scope). Duration often reflects
different aims, very short-term projects focused on
social experimentation (King’s Cross, Caserres) versus
semi-permanent interventions designed to support
urban transitions (Tempelhof, Aspern).

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Innovative facade solutions for energy
efficiency in contemporary architecture:
inspired by traditional elements

Ruşen Ergün

Department of Architecture, Faculty of Architecture, Dicle University, Diyarbakır, Turkey.
Email:   rusen.ergun@dicle.edu.tr

Abstract: The reinterpretation of traditional passive design strategies is of importance to modern architectural
sustainability. One such effective strategy, widely used in traditional architecture to achieve indoor thermal
comfort, is mashrabiya. The aim of this study is to evaluate the effect of adapting mashrabiya to modern
architecture on energy-efficient facade design. The city of Diyarbakır, located in the hot and dry climate zone
of Turkey, was selected as the study area. The mashrabiya is integrated into the living room. In this context,
the effect of the use of perforated dimensions, depth and components of the mashrabiya on the indoor cooling
load on four different facades was analysed. As a result of 148 different analyses, the use of mashrabiya on
the south, east and west facades was found to be an important strategy. The use of appropriate perforation
sizes, depths and components in the mashrabiya reduced the cooling load by approximately 21% on the south
facade, 17% on the west facade and 13% on the east facade. This study shows the importance of mashrabiya
in modern buildings for indoor thermal comfort. This is considered an important guideline for sustainable
building in this context.

Keywords: Sustainability; Mashrabia; Traditional architecture; Innovative facade; Hot and dry climate.

Received: 10/04/2025; Accepted: 29/07/2025; Available online: 31/10/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: Ergün, R. (2025). ‘Innovative facade solutions for energy efficiency in contemporary architecture: inspired by traditional elements’. VITRUVIO -
International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e23711. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.23711

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

AI-driven transformation of traditional
architectural Ottoman style into modern
mosque designs

Ragd AlHarbat  *, Aref Maksoud  , Leen Alsyouf  , Mohamed Alali

Department of Architectural Engineering, University of Sharjah, Sharjah, UAE.
\*Email:   u23107025@sharjah.ac.ae

Abstract: The use of AI art-generating platforms has recently become a trend in different disciplines, including
the field of architecture. The relation AI has with architecture led to the idea of this paper to explore the
intersection between these platforms with traditional Islamic architecture, specifically the Ottoman style.
To achieve a proper evaluation, this paper discusses the use of 6 different platforms to generate 18 images
using the text-to-image method and by employing 3 distinct prompts from different complexities to guide the
generation process. Moreover, a comparative analysis conducted to evaluate the generated images using 2
criteria, the first assesses the Ottoman architectural elements that appeared in the images such as the domes,
minarets, and arches. Then the second investigates the overall excellence of the figures considering factors
such as realism, quality and resolution, overall details, and lastly the consistency with the given prompt. The
results demonstrate that across all the 6 platforms, the more detailed the prompt is, the better the outcome.
However, Midjourney was the only platform that was able to produce all three images consistently from
the first prompt with all the required elements, and as the prompts got more detailed, the other platforms
provided more accurate results.

Keywords: AI art platforms; image generation; architecture; Ottoman style; mosque design.

Received: 13/05/2025; Accepted: 03/10/2025; Available online: 07/11/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: AlHarbat, R., Maksoud, A, Alsyouf, L., Alali, M. (2025). ‘AI-driven transformation of traditional architectural Ottoman style into modern mosque
designs’. VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e23933. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.23933

Adding to this, the paper aims to investigate the differ-
ences between a couple of AI art platforms regarding the
understanding of the Islamic architectural elements, the
response to the inputted prompt, and the effectiveness of
the quality of the generated images. Therefore, this paper
seeks to answer the following research question: What is
the most effective AI art platform that understands the
elements and characteristics of the Ottoman architectural
style, and how do different AI art platforms incorporate a
modern aesthetic into these traditional elements in the
generated images?

• Presenting a comprehensive summary of the OS, its
characteristics, and the unique features of this style,
especially in the traditional mosque design.

results as an AI art platform through a comparative
analysis with five other platforms.

\3. Results and discussion

3.1. Results and discussion of the Architectural
elements comparison

3.1.1. Results and discussion of prompt 1

Starting with the first image in Table 4, and Figure 2 gener-
ated using Midjourney, the result clearly elaborates on
the main elements of Ottoman architecture. The main
dome is large, central, and flanked by smaller domes. In
addition, the multiple minarets are placed on each corner
and inherited the characteristics of the Ottoman minaret,
using reasonably tall and pencil-shaped minarets with
balconies. Finally, the arches are all pointed, therefore
strongly reflecting the OS.

Moving on to the second image generated using
Runway in Figure 3, the result partly reflects the OS in
terms of the main elements, yet lacks some details. The
dome is large and central, but does not have any smaller Figure 2 | The generated image for prompt 1 using Midjourney.
domes flanking it. Two minarets, both pencil-shaped
and arguably tall in respect to the overall proportion,
with a balcony reflecting the OS well. On the other hand,
the arches are random and inconsistent. Multiple types
of arches were used, such as the pointed arch and the
horseshoe pointed, therefore not reflecting the OS.

The next generated image in Figure 4 is by Leonardo
and does not reflect the distinctive elements of the OS
strongly. The dome is central but takes a different shape
from the Ottoman domes and has a ring of windows
around the bottom. The minarets are large but dispro-
portionate and partly express the OS as they are pencil-
shaped but grow larger in diameter going down. The
arches demonstrate an effort to achieve a pointed form;
nevertheless, this is not completely achieved, thereby
weakening their architectural integrity to the OS.

The image generated by DreamStudio is shown
in Figure 5 reveals that the dome had some Ottoman
characteristics since it was large and central, but it was
not flanked by smaller domes or half domes. Moreover,
the minarets had a distinctive Ottoman design since they Figure 3 | The generated image for prompt 1 using Runway.
were tall, had balconies, a pencil shape, and there were
multiple ones. Lastly, the arch style was not reflective of
OS because it was not pointed, but a pointed horseshoe
arch. The last image in this Table 4 shown in Figure 7 is related
to the Playground platform, and it was able to give notice-
On the other hand, the image generated by Dalle-3 able, distinctive Ottoman elements successfully regarding
in Figure 6 had a distinctive Ottoman design for both the both the arch style and the multiple minarets because they

3.2.2. Results and discussion of prompt 2

The second group of prompts in Table 9 and Figure 8
generated by Midjourney failed to provide a realistic
render. The outcome was affected by the interpretation of
the word “pencil” in the prompt, which was intended to
specify a pencil-shaped minaret but was misconstrued as
referring to a pencil drawing. The outcome turned out to
be close to a colored pencil sketch rather than a photo-
realistic rendering. Other than that, the result showed
particularly good overall details and image resolution,
and the quality is high. Although the consistency with the
prompt had the potential to be high, but it was lowered
due to the misinterpretation of the word “pencil” as
mentioned previously.

<!-- p. 99 -->

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Current relevance of the historic
construction technique of Bousillage
using Spanish moss, golden broom, and
Posidonia oceanica

Ana Patricia Minguito García

Universidad Politécnica de Madrid, Department of Architectural Composition, Spain.
Email: ap.minguito@upm.es

Abstract: Bousillage is a traditional construction technique used to build vertical and horizontal enclosures
in tropical, West European, and Mediterranean regions. It uses a timbered structure called colombage and
a fibrous filler made from natural waste. This research paper confirms the current practice of this ancient
technique by analyzing its structural, hydrothermal and environmental peculiarities through the examination
of three persistent solutions that use golden broom, Spanish moss, and Posidonia oceanica as binders. The
methodology begins with a theoretical framework that confirms the technique’s historical roots according to
context. Then, field and hygrothermal data is collected for each vegetal binder type. The main objective is to
test the initial hypothesis that bousillage is a symbiotic construction method which creates a balance between
nature and anthropocentrism in the local ecosystem—regardless of the plant waste used or the climatic
context in which it is developed. The research results clarify that this environmental balance is achieved
when waste discarded by nature is used as an active component in thermal building improvements. This
benefits both the fauna and flora species, as well as anthropized neighborhoods. In conclusion, reviving this
traditional technique in accordance with the current ecological situation, creates necessary anthroponatural
interdependence for the long-term survival of the ecosystem.

Keywords: bioinsulation; framework; binder; filler; environment; balance.

Received: 27/06/2025; Accepted: 04/11/2025; Available online: 21/11/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.
Cite as: Minguito García, A.P. (2025). ‘Current relevance of the historic construction technique of Bousillage using Spanish moss,
golden broom, and Posidonia oceanica’. VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24209.
https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24209

\4. Discussion of results

SM, GB, and PO exhibit sustainability dynamics that
significantly impact three stages: their role as atmospheric regulators, their reutilization process that benefits
nature by managing its waste, and their ability to achieve
high hygrothermal behavior.

4.1. A unified environmental approach

they produce tiny tendrils that anchor it to the tree as

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Conscious architecture and circular
economy for a transition in building:
The IMIP project as a case study

Salvador Gilabert-Sanz  *1, José Vicente Oliver-Villanueva  2

1Universitat Politècnica de Catalunya Barcelona Tech, UPC, Spain.
2Universitat Politècnica de València, UPV, Spain.
\*Email:   salvador.gilabert@upc.edu

Abstract: This research explores the role of design in fostering sustainable transitions through systemic
thinking in the building sector, addressing the dynamic, multi-scalar, multi-species, and transdisciplinary
challenges inherent in socio-ecological and socio-technical transformations. The study is grounded in
Transition Design principles, recognizing the agency of ecological, human, and computational intelligence.
By embracing systemic approaches, this paper underscores the importance of long-term thinking, collective
action, pluralistic perspectives, and value co-creation among diverse actors. The results highlight the IMIP
(Innovative Eco-Construction System Based on Interlocking Modular Insulation Wood & Cork-Based Panels)
project’s contribution to circular economy principles, demonstrating its environmental and social impact
through a comprehensive Life Cycle Assessment (LCA), as an innovative holistic approach to low-energy
construction and near-zero-emissions strategies in construction, leveraging local timber and cork as materials
for construction systems in building. These results demonstrate the potential of locally based bio-based
materials and modular construction systems to promote circularity, reduce ecological footprints, and enhance
community resilience. The findings reinforce the significance of design as a driver of systemic change in
the building sector, emphasizing the interdependencies and feedback loops between human actions, natural
ecosystems, and technological infrastructures.

Keywords: Conscious architecture; sustainable construction; circular economy; cork insulation; life cycle
assessment.

Received: 07/07/2025; Accepted: 03/10/2025; Available online: 07/11/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: Gilabert-Sanz, S., Oliver-Villanueva, J.V. (2025). ‘Conscious architecture and circular economy for a transition in building: The IMIP project as a
case study’. VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24284. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24284

VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

Industrialization for sustainable facade
rehabilitation: a feasibility study in aging
urban environments

Milagro Iborra-Lucas  *, John Jairo Zambrano Alcívar

Dept. of Architectural Constructions, Universitat Politècnica de València.
\*Email:   miborra1@upvnet.upv.es

Abstract: The existing building stock represents one of the main contributors to environmental impact, as
a large portion of constructions were built under outdated regulations and technical requirements with
low energy efficiency. This study assesses the technical feasibility of incorporating industrialized façade
systems as an energy rehabilitation strategy, with particular attention to their structural compatibility
under additional loads and their potential to enhance the thermal performance of older buildings.
Through a case study, structural and energy simulation tools were applied to compare different modular
prototypes. The results demonstrate that it is possible to integrate industrialized solutions compatible with
existing structures, achieving substantial improvements in both energy performance and building habitability.
Industrialization in rehabilitation enables the modernization of obsolete buildings and represents a viable
alternative with reduced environmental impact. This approach provides objective technical criteria to support
decision-making in rehabilitation projects, contributing to extending the service life of the building stock and
aligning it with sustainability goals.

Keywords: energy rehabilitation; industrialized façades; modular systems; energy efficiency; technical
feasibility; urban environments.

Received: 16/10/2025; Accepted: 12/12/2025; Available online: 23/12/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: Iborra-Lucas, M., Zambrano Alcívar, J.J. (2025). ‘Industrialization for sustainable facade rehabilitation: a feasibility study in aging urban
environments’. VITRUVIO - International Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24825. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24825

EXISTING FAÇADE
RETAIN REMOVE
Savings in demolition and waste management costs. Greater freedom to customize and apply a modular system.
Reduction in on-site intervention time. Preservation of structural safety and stability of the building.
Overcomes initial energy performance limitations. Compliance with current energy efficiency standards.
Reduces risks associated with interior intrusion. Lower maintenance requirements in the future.
May require structural reinforcement (depending on the case). Generation of construction waste.
Limited customization possibilities. Longer intervention periods.
Possible encroachment into public space due to façade extension. Potential damage resulting from demolition.
Alteration of the building’s original architectural characteristics. Higher costs in terms of labor, time, and waste management.

\4. Selection of the case study

The project under study corresponds to a mixed-use
building combining residential and commercial func-
tions, located within a consolidated urban environment.
Constructed in the second half of the 20th century, in
1973, it represents an architectural typology characteristic
of that period. The building was designed in accordance
with the Reinforced Concrete Code EH-73 and, regarding
thermal conditions in buildings, the NBE CT-79 standard.
For the development of the analysis, two BIM-based
applications were employed: CYPECAD, used for the
design, calculation, and sizing of building and civil engi-
neering structures, and CYPETHERM HE Plus, used for the
certification of building energy efficiency and for verifying
compliance with CTE DB HE 1 (Limitation of Energy
Demand). Both tools enabled a detailed evaluation of the
structural behaviour and the energy performance of the
model, ensuring the reliability and precision of the results
obtained.

\5. Simulation and estimation of maximum
admissible load

The analysis focused on the façade areas intended for
the installation of the industrialized modular system
(Figure 6). For this purpose, the structural capacity
was assessed by progressively applying different load
combinations, which made it possible to determine the
maximum admissible load at each point and, ultimately,
identify the failure threshold. As an initial reference,
existing modular systems were considered, with weights
ranging between 45 and 70 kg/m² and thermal transmit-
tance values between 0.21 and 0.30 W/m²K.

under conservative conditions. This value was applied
to simulate both static and dynamic environmental

able loads such as wind and the self-weight of the system. blocks. Finally, the relevant actions affecting the structure

\7. Economic and social feasibility

In rehabilitation projects, feasibility depends on
balancing higher initial costs with medium- and long-
term benefits. Although industrialized systems require
greater upfront investment compared to conventional
techniques, this additional cost is offset by the signifi-
cant reduction in execution times and the minimization
of indirect expenses. Moreover, the estimated return on Figure 9 | CYPETHERM Model. Source: by the authors.
investment (ROI) demonstrates that progressive energy
savings and the resulting increase in property value
allow for the recovery of the initial expenditure within
a reasonable timeframe. This reinforces the strategic

Methodology for LCA Assessment of Heritage
Buildings and their retrofitting works

Belén Onecha *1, Patricio Suárez 2

1Barcelona School of Architecture. Universitat Politècnica de Catalunya, Spain.
2Universitat Politècnica de Catalunya, Spain.
\*Email:   belen.onecha@upc.edu

Abstract: In the current context of necessary reduction of environmental impact, retrofitting historical
buildings is essential, but it must be specified the total carbon footprint, considering the original construction
and the current restoration.
This research has a double objective: firstly, to develop a methodology to establish the carbon footprint of
three historic Catalan Modernist buildings at the time of their construction; and secondly, to determine the
carbon footprint of the restoration works of one of them.
An exhaustive analysis is carried on about the materials used, their extraction, transportation and
manufacturing methods at the time. Following the methodological structure of contemporary EPDs and LCA,
both aligned with the UNE-EN 15804 standard. This ensures methodological consistency between historical
and modern datasets and provides a robust basis for comparison.
Then, the embodied carbon of these buildings is compared with that of modern buildings construction, in
order to establish which is higher. Finally, the carbon footprint of retrofitting one of them is also calculated.
The importance of this research lies in the methodology for the quantification of the total environmental
impact of historic buildings, from their original construction to their restoration. The limitation of the study
lies in the fact that only one of the three case studies has been retrofitted so far.

Keywords: Heritage; environmental impact; carbon footprint; Catalan Modernism; Gaudí.

Received: 09/09/2025; Accepted: 05/12/2025; Available online: 22/12/2025; Published: 31/12/2025.

Cite as: Onecha, B., & Suárez, P. (2025). ‘Methodology for LCA Assessment of Heritage Buildings and their retrofitting works’. VITRUVIO - International
Journal of Architectural Technology and Sustainability, 10(2), e24604. https://doi.org/10.4995/vitruvio-ijats.2025.24604

This section illustrates the results obtained in the series
of investigations described above (2.1 and 2.3). In this
table (Table 4), we can see the results of the historical
embodied carbon research shown in the second column,
as noted above, data sources include museums and
factory archives and secondary literature on the matter:
(Arup, 2023; Baila Lletí, 2012; Berg, 2021; Betevé, 2015; Bing

Using these values, the total carbon footprint for the
construction of the three selected heritage buildings was
calculated.

3.2. Embodied carbon in case studies

Material usage across the three projects was relatively
similar, with the greatest variation observed in the use
of industrial materials, which were not yet widely distributed in residential construction. When unknown, materials were assumed to be of local or national origin. The
results of each case are illustrated in Charts 02, 03 and 04,
where beige represents the percentage of the material
used in relation to the total amount of materials used in
the construction, and orange, its percentage of embodied
carbon.

The average embodied carbon across the three case
studies was calculated at 467.87 kgCO₂/m², slightly higher
than the modern average of 432.66 kgCO₂/m² (as shown
in Chart 05). However, these modern figures cover a wide
range of building typologies (as shown in Chart 07) and
should not be viewed as definitive. Continued research
and comparable case studies will enable more precise
benchmarking.

These findings suggest that while production
processes—particularly for concrete and steel—have
become more efficient, the average carbon footprint
remains stable due to the introduction of numerous
modern materials such as plastics, aluminium, alloys,
synthetic insulators, etc. Thus, maintaining a similar footprint while improving building performance represents
notable progress. For instance, a separate study (González
& García, 2006) demonstrated that sustainable construction practices in Spain reduced embodied carbon by 30%.

Concrete and steel remain the most used materials,
consuming four times less energy today than a century
ago (Smil 2017), followed by glass at 3.4 times less.
While these improvements are significant, further innovation and alternative materials are necessary. Chart 05
compares the material volume used in the study cases
in beige and its embodied carbon percentage in orange,
highlighting the difference between one value and the
other in non-industrialized and industrialized materials
(although inverted) and the more stable relationship in
semi-industrialized or low-energy consumption materials. In green, it shows the material lifespan, with 100% Charts 02, 03, 04 | Embodied carbon in each study case. Own Making,
equivalent to 1,000 years. This highlights the durability of based on data and sources listed in Table 1

traditional materials such as brick, and the relative imper-
manence of others, which require ongoing maintenance
and eventual replacement.

3.3. Intervention Analysis: A Minimal Restoration
Scenario

The embodied carbon values of original and modern
materials are detailed in Chart 06 as follows: in orange
the original incorporated carbon and in green the
added one, including new materials. This highlights
the amount of carbon saved. This targeted intervention
increased the building’s carbon footprint by just 9.12%
compared to its original value. In other words, full habit-
ability was restored with less than 10% of the original
emissions. When compared to the average emissions of
new construction (444 kgCO₂/m²), the restoration gener-
ated only 7.92% of those emissions, exceeding the 40%
savings target set by the World Green Building Council.
As mentioned above, 6 modern cases were taken for
comparison, this is shown in Chart 07, where the average
embodied carbon of the three study cases is compared
Chart 05 | (Above), Average embodied carbon. Own Making, based on to the six modern cases. The results indicate that the
data and sources listed in Table 1 and Charts 02, 03 and 04. embodied carbon is stable through time, except in case
4, since they were homes specifically designed to use
low-energy intensive materials (González & García, 2006).
﻿ This is important because it underlines the need to
continue searching for low-impact solutions in construc-
tion, but also shows that we have managed to improve
the comfort features of buildings while maintaining the
same carbon footprint.

\4. Conclusion and Discussion

In the introduction of this article, it has become clear that
retrofitting is better than constructing a new building
in life cycle terms, but also the need to provide reliable
historical and constructive data and criteria guidance
for intervention, resulting in a better understanding of
the energy and carbon impact of any retrofitting process
(Huang et al., 2025). In sum, a method for LCA assessment
prior to refurbishment to understand the environmental
impact of proposed solutions (Sesana et al., 2019), as
well as a policy and certification schemes to measure
life-cycle impacts and to propose low-impact refurbish-
ment strategies that are sensitive to heritage buildings
(Wise et al., 2019).

If buildings professionals need help to achieve
sustainability objectives, architectural heritage profes-
sionals even more so, as they have to be able to detect
heritage values and propose sustainable actions, this is a
Chart 06 | (Below), Casa Pilar Moragas original and added embodied
carbon.

15 V

<!-- p. 163 -->

Methodology for LCA Assessment of Heritage Buildings and their retrofitting works VITRUVIO 10 | 2 (2025)
Onecha and Suárez International Journal of Architecture
Technology and Sustainability

﻿
overall footprint. It should be noted that these materials
were not a discovery of the Industrial Revolution, and that
their manufacture before it was even more demanding
of energy and resources, however, it was at this time
that their use became widespread as they became
cheaper and more massive. Therefore, they appeared in
greater quantities in this period than in previous ones. In
short, building at the dawn of the 20th century is not as
sustainable as is commonly claimed. Embedded carbon
is inferior for contemporary construction; if we currently
pollute more, it is because of the volume of materials and
energy we consume per person, as it is estimated that we
use four times more energy per capita (Smil, 2017).

There are three types of results for the materials anal-
ysed when we compare their embodied carbon and the
volume that each value represents in the total construc-
tion, as we have already seen in chart 05:

\1. Stone, sand and wood. As they do not undergo
industrial transformation processes, their footprint is
minimal, and as they are widely used in this type of
construction, the volume/ incorporated carbon ratio
is very high.
